Dự báo lãi suất 2026 
Dự báo mặt bằng lãi suất sẽ ổn định 
ở vùng hiện tại trong quý IV

Với xu hướng tăng nóng của mặt bằng lãi suất kể từ đầu năm đã bắt đầu gây ra 
ảnh hưởng tiêu cực tới hoạt động sản xuất kinh doanh của nhiều doanh nghiệp 
và sức cầu của nền kinh tế, Chính phủ và NHNN nhiều khả năng sẽ có các biện 
pháp can thiệp giúp bình ổn hoặc hạ nhiệt nhẹ mặt bằng lãi suất trong quý IV. 
Một số biện pháp bao gồm thực hiện tái cấp vốn chỉ định cho các định chế tài 
chính rót vốn vào lĩnh vực ưu tiên, và nới lỏng cơ chế giúp các NHTM huy động 
vốn quốc tế thông qua phát hành trái phiếu ra thị trường nước ngoài. Đồng 
thời, chúng tôi kỳ vọng giải ngân đầu tư công sẽ tiếp tục được đẩy mạnh trong 
những tháng cuối năm, qua đó thúc đẩy dòng tiền quay trở lại nền kinh tế và 
hỗ trợ thanh khoản hệ thống.

Tuy nhiên, để các biện pháp trên phát huy hiệu quả rõ nét và lan tỏa rộng hơn, 
đặc biệt đối với lãi suất huy động và cho vay ở các kỳ hạn dài, vẫn cần chờ tín 
hiệu hạ nhiệt rõ nét của giá dầu (điều chỉnh xuống vùng 80 USD/thùng hoặc 
thấp hơn). Khi đó, áp lực lạm phát giảm bớt, tạo điều kiện thuận lợi hơn để 
NHNN thực hiện điều tiết thanh khoản và hỗ trợ mặt bằng lãi suất giảm một 
cách bền vững.

### Page 18

18

Tỷ giá  
  Tỷ giá 3Q2026 – Đà giảm duy trì, áp lực không lớn 
Diễn biến tỷ giá tiếp tục ổn định, 
bất chấp biến động bên ngoài 
 Xu hướng giảm của tỷ giá USD/VND đã xuất hiện rõ nét kể từ giữa quý III, bất 
chấp chỉ số DXY tiếp tục neo cao quanh 99-102 điểm và Fed thực hiện thắt chặt 
chính sách tiền tệ trong tháng 9. Tính riêng trong tháng 9, tỷ giá dao động trong 
vùng 25,918-26,044 đồng, tương đương với mức giảm 1-1.4% so với cuối 2025. 
Nguyên nhân cho sự cải thiện của VND đến từ:  
(1) Nguồn cung ngoại tệ chảy gia tăng nhờ ba động lực chính: (i) giải ngân 
FDI duy trì khả quan (lũy kế 9T2026 đạt 21.07 tỷ USD, +12.1% YoY); (ii) 
cán cân thương mại tháng 9 chuyển biến tích cực với mức xuất siêu 
1.27 tỷ USD, lần đầu tiên ghi nhận thặng dư trong năm 2026; và (iii) 
hoạt động huy động vốn quốc tế của khối ngân hàng và doanh nghiệp 
tiếp tục được triển khai, với một số khoản vay hợp vốn quy mô lớn như 
HDBank (721 triệu USD) và VinFast (200 triệu USD). 
(2) Chênh lệch lãi suất VND–USD liên ngân hàng trung dài hạn (1W-6M) 
vẫn duy trì trạng thái dương, làm tăng chi phí cơ hội nắm giữ USD và 
giúp hạn chế hoạt động đầu cơ ngoại tệ.

Trên thị trường tự do, tỷ giá tiếp nối đà giảm của quý trước, duy trì ở mức thấp 
hơn tỷ giá liên ngân hàng trong tháng 8 và 9. Tính đến hết ngày 5/10/2026, tỷ 
giá tự do giảm 2.9% so với cuối năm 2025, về mức 26,030 đồng. Diễn biến này 
được hỗ trợ bởi các chính sách nhằm tăng cường quản lý thị trường ngoại tệ, 
vàng và tài sản mã hóa, qua đó hạn chế nhu cầu ngoại tệ cho hoạt động trên thị 
trường tự do. Dưới sự kiểm soát chặt chẽ của NHNN, chênh lệch giữa giá vàng 
nội địa và thế giới đã thu hẹp đáng kể trong thời gian trở lại đây về mức 5-10 
triệu VND/lượng, từ mức 20-30 triệu VND/lượng trong quý đầu năm.

NEER và REER diễn biến trái chiều

Theo mô hình tính toán của chúng tôi, NEER - tỷ giá danh nghĩa đa phương đo 
lường giá trị của đồng VND với rổ 8 đồng tiền tham chiếu theo cơ chế tỷ giá 
trung tâm và REER - tỷ giá thực đa phương là chỉ số được điều chỉnh lạm phát so 
với NEER. Tính đến cuối quý III/2026, NEER đảo chiều giảm nhẹ 0.4% so với cuối 
năm 2025, phản ánh đồng VND đã giảm giá danh nghĩa so với rổ tiền tệ của các 
đối tác thương mại. Ở chiều ngược lại, REER vẫn ghi nhận mức tăng so với cuối 
2025 (+1.5% YTD), dù có xu hướng thu hẹp so với quý trước. Điều này cho thấy 
VND vẫn đang lên giá theo giá trị thực, hàm ý sức cạnh tranh về giá của hàng 
hóa Việt Nam có phần suy giảm.

Dự báo tỷ giá 2026  
Dự báo tỷ giá USD/VND tương đối 
ổn định trong quý IV

Chúng tôi cho rằng rủi ro đối với triển vọng tỷ giá USD/VND trong quý IV là hiện 
hữu khi xung đột Mỹ - Iran kéo dài hơn dự kiến, cùng việc dự trữ xăng dầu chiến 
lược của Mỹ suy giảm xuống mức báo động, sẽ đẩy giá năng lượng lên cao. Diễn 
biến này làm gia tăng áp lực lạm phát tại Mỹ, qua đó buộc Fed phải duy trì 
chính sách tiền tệ thắt chặt lâu hơn và kích hoạt đà tăng của chỉ số DXY trong 
bối cảnh nền kinh tế nước này vẫn đang có khả năng chống chịu tương đối tốt.

Dù vậy, chúng tôi dự báo tỷ giá USD/VND cuối năm 2026 sẽ đi ngang so với đầu 
năm, trong kịch bản cơ sở giá dầu bình quân quý IV ở mức 90-95 USD/thùng, với 
các yếu tố hỗ trợ bao gồm: 
(1) Chỉ số DXY khó có diễn biến tăng mạnh trong thời gian tới khi nợ công 
Mỹ tiếp tục gia tăng làm suy giảm sức hấp dẫn của đồng USD và dự báo 
đồng thuận cho thấy các NHTW lướn khác cũng đang có xu hướng thắt 
chặt chính sách tiền tệ để kiểm soát lạm phát. Chúng tôi dự báo chỉ số 
DXY sẽ biến động trong vùng 98 - 103 điểm (tham khảo thêm tại báo 
cáo Triển vọng TTCK 4Q2026).

Report Introduction
Our proprietary dbDataInsights survey shows cyber threats are evolving faster than organisations can adapt
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.
“It’s the only thing that can shut down my business.”
That was the punchline from a conversation I had recently with the CEO of a large
privately held financial services company. This was his comment when I followed up

as to why cybersecurity risk is the number one concern that “keeps him up at night”.
To help understand this risk, our dbDataInsights team surveyed more than 50
cybersecurity experts. The uncomfortable conclusion is that many firms are
unprepared for cyber threats. Cyber risk is increasing as AI adoption accelerates.
—89% agree the threat landscape is evolving faster than organisations can adapt.
—78% say the gap between highly and poorly prepared organisations is widening.

What has changed with AI advancement? Cyberattacks have become faster,
cheaper, more deceptive and more scalable. Capabilities once reserved for
sophisticated actors are now broadly accessible to a new generation of profit-driven
criminals. At the same time, rising geopolitical tension has made cyber operations
an increasingly attractive low-cost, deniable tool for nation states. Corporates and
investors therefore need to understand their broader exposure and critical

infrastructure risk in their supply chain and with stakeholders.
Among other autonomous incidents, the recent Hugging Face incident
highlighted AI’s cyberattack potential. AI agents caused harm without
direction from a human attacker, with weak guardrails and no human
judgement to restrain them. Beyond the systemic questions it raised, the attack
highlights three additional concerns: (i) AI operates at a speed and scale humans
cannot match, with agents taking over 17,000+ actions in four days; (ii) the intrusion

began in the data-processing pipeline, where AI platforms are uniquely exposed;
and (iii) the agents escaped their test environment through a widely used third-party
tool representing supplier risk in an unfamiliar form.

### Page 4

Report summary and notable takeaways
Secular trends and AI adoption have widened the attack surface; the severity of losses is compounding
Source: FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), company security advisories, OECD, company reports, Deutsche Bank Research.
Secular trends have widened the attack surface. This includes rapid
digitalisation, widespread Internet of Things (IoT) endpoints, hybrid workforces,
cloud adoption and deeper supply chain dependencies. The scale of the threat

increase is reflected in data from the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3),
which shows cybercrime complaints more than doubled from 2019 pre-pandemic
baseline of ~467,000 to ~1.0mn in 2025, with a 3x expansion relative to 2017 levels.
Barriers to entry are lower; attack tools are now sold “off the shelf”. Today,
cybercrime marketplaces, available on the dark web, look less like the underworld
portrayed in films and more like an online software store. Buyers can access AI-

powered tools that scan for software vulnerabilities (see top-right chart showing
reported CVEs after the release of cyber capable frontier models), write malicious
code at speed, and provide access to malware that rewrites its own code to avoid
detection as it runs. Ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) takes it further, renting out
ready-made attack kits, often in exchange for a portion of the profits.
Impersonation is becoming harder to detect. Hackers can now generate more

realistic phishing messages, while voice cloning and deepfakes make impersonation
convincing enough to gain access unnoticed. With stolen credentials, attackers often
aren’t hacking in, they are simply logging in.
The rise of loose cybercriminal collectives. Some of the most damaging recent
attacks have been linked to loosely organised, often English-speaking networks such
as ShinyHunters and Scattered Spider. The latter has been widely named as the

group behind the 2025 Marks & Spencer cyberattack, in which the hacking group
deployed RaaS. AI is enabling these groups to become larger and faster, widening
the gap between the speed of attack and a defender’s response.
The severity of losses is compounding. A 2026 IBM study puts the average breach
at a record USD ~5.0mn, up 12% YoY, but the impact on large companies can run
into hundreds of millions or even billions. Estimates for global cyber insurance

premiums (per Munich Re) point to a rise from roughly USD 7.0bn in 2020 to USD
15.0bn in 2025, with expectations to reach USD 28.0bn by 2030.

### Page 5

5
Among the more concerning survey takeaways is that a large-scale and
significant systemic incident is now the base case.
— 83% expect a significant systemic attack within two years and 81% expect it to be
moderately, very, or extremely severe for economic activity and markets.
— 81% say AI favours attackers today, with not one respondent putting defenders
ahead of attackers. Only 20% expect defenders to be ahead in five years.
Geopolitical tensions coupled with rising cyber capabilities have transformed

cyberspace into a digital battlefield between global powers. Intent has moved
towards espionage and disruption of not only critical infrastructure (e.g., healthcare,
finance, power, industrial systems, defence) but private companies as well. Chinese
President Xi Jinping has linked cybersecurity to national security and economic and
social stability, with China’s public statements similarly emphasising the protection
of critical infrastructure. At the same time, the US’s June 2025 Executive Order on

Cybersecurity reflects its continued focus on national cyber resilience.
Europe relies on US cyber tools while its start-ups lag in funding. Reliance on US
security vendors exposes European firms to potential access, update or export
restrictions over the long-term. Shrinking this gap will prove difficult given US cyber
start-ups attract nearly 6x the venture and growth funding of European peers. And
while the timing of quantum computing (which could break widely used encryption

methods) remains uncertain, it could further deepen Europe’s dependency.
Ultimately, cyber preparedness is a financial variable. Investors should ask what
firms spend on security versus AI adoption, how well they know their vulnerabilities,
and how fast they can recover.
— Only 24% of respondents think organisations have sufficient visibility into the
threats relevant to them, even though 57% saw cybersecurity budgets rise over
the past 12 months.

— 81% agree that cybersecurity related laws and regulation cannot keep pace, and
65% doubt leadership understands cyber risk well enough.

### Page 6

6
When cyber weakness = share price consequence…
Investors are more aware of cyber risk today, but they rarely sell a stock due to
perceived cyber vulnerabilities (although we are aware of some who have).
—What is the potential impact? Our dbDataInsights event study of 47 breaches
shows a cumulative breach-attributed return of c. -2.9% within the first two
trading days and c. -6.0% through first 20 days trading days. The erosion then
continues more gradually, reaching c. -6.7% nine months after disclosure (see

slide 13).
Which industries are most vulnerable?
—Our survey respondents point to (1) government & public sector (52%), (2)
financial services (50%), (3) energy and utilities (46%) and (4) healthcare and life
sciences follow (39%, rising to 48% among US respondents).
We should caveat, that high sector exposure does not mean every company in it is
equally vulnerable. Some firms in these “exposed” sectors may very well be better
prepared given they have built specialist teams and defence protocols for decades.

What about access to credit and ratings? Credit and rating providers are paying
attention. Moody’s and S&P both cut their outlook on Jaguar Land Rover within two
months of its 2025 hack. Today, this type of impact is not widespread. But if risk
escalates through a systemic incident or an AI-driven step change in attacks these
mechanisms, including ESG ratings, could reprice asset ownership or impact access
to credit. In fact, some lenders are already pricing it. Journal of Corporate Finance

research using US syndicated loan data found borrowers with persistently higher cyber
risk pay 4–13bp more.
The bottom line? While a broader systemic attack is hard to prepare for, corporates
and investors should be asking questions about vulnerabilities, security investment,
preparedness and recovery should an attack occur. This is especially true for
industries quickly adopting AI capabilities and / or a high threat landscape.

dbDataInsights Cybersecurity Expert Survey methodology
—This survey was conducted among 54 cybersecurity professionals across the US
and Europe in September of 2026. Questions took the form of either multiple

choice selections or free form responses. The dbDataInsights team received the
responses anonymously, but respondents were required to work or consult in
cyber security roles
—Who took the survey? On self-identified roles, around 80% sit inside an
organisation’s cybersecurity function, with the remainder working independently
(15%) or in academia and research (6%). The most common titles were security
architect (23%), Chief Information Security Officer (16%) and security analyst (9%),

alongside Head of Security, Deputy CISO, Incident Response Lead and Security
Operations Lead.
—Only a small minority of respondents work for cyber resellers, thus survey views
are primarily attributable to those working within an organization on
cybersecurity.
—Survey participants were also asked for their perspectives on cyber resilience and
governance, what is misunderstood, alongside emerging threats, risk to AI
adoption and the influence of geopolitics.

—While responses were anonymous, in some cases we conducted follow up
interviews with certain participants where contact details were made available.
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.

### Page 10

10
Risk from AI-enabled attacks and added AI adoption vulnerabilities
Insider threats and supply chain compromises remain a growing concern
“The fear of AI writing undetectable malware is … overblown. Attackers were
never short of malware, since they can buy it ready made along with initial
access. Good detection looks at behaviour rather than exact code, so a rewritten
payload still has to do the same suspicious things.”
“Frontier AI models are currently the main hype. Automated phishing and

scams are underappreciated, yet they are responsible for the bigger
monetary losses at the moment.”
“There are too many organisations relying on the same providers for
critical services and this means single points of failure.”
“A corporation might be very secure but if they rely on a third party that
does not have the same level of security posture, [it] could be
compromised via this third-party (basically their weak link).”
“Existing employee login behaviour and usage of AI is underappreciated –

it’s what poses the biggest threat to a breach.”
“No company can guarantee they won’t be breached, so there needs to
be more focus put on the ability to recover and survive an attack.”
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research .

### Page 11

11
Experts believe governments are not keeping pace, and no one owns AI oversight
How experts surveyed by dbDataInsights view governance and regulation
—AI adoption is outpacing internal controls. As one expert put it: “Companies
are pushing the use of AI more to their employees without the proper
mechanisms and policies in place to prevent misuse or attacks initiated by the AI
systems themselves.”
—Regulation is struggling to keep pace. 81% of respondents agree that

cybersecurity-related laws and regulations are failing to keep up with
technological change, and 54% say regulatory requirements can improve security
but may also slow innovation. One respondent highlighted “the levels of
uncertainty, the long running nature of the problems, and the rapidity of change.”
—Responsibility for AI oversight remains unsettled. International bodies are the
leading choice, supported by 50% of respondents. No option commands a

majority, underscoring the lack of consensus on who should oversee AI
development.
—Better board questions may not guarantee better decisions. The survey
points to more informed questioning by corporate leadership, yet 65% doubt that
leadership teams understand cyber risk well enough to make informed business
trade-offs. Measuring activity more effectively than outcomes further complicates
oversight.
—Accountability is becoming personal. Only 43% say accountability for cyber risk

is clearly defined in their governance framework, yet the consequences now reach
the top as a handful of CEOs have left or gone into early retirement after
financially consequential cyber breaches.
Who should primarily oversee AI development?
dbDataInsights survey – September 2026, based on overseeing development while managing cyber risk

Material cyber breaches can cause a notable drop in share price
Our dbDataInsights study found a 2.9% drop on average within two trading days, with the cumulative breach-attributed return of -6.0% on trading day 20.

Source: dbDataInsights, Bloomberg Finance LP., Deutsche Bank Research, Company filings. Event study of reported cybersecurity incidents, 2015 -2025, 34 US and 13 European companies across nine sectors.
What typically happens to a company’s shares when a breach is reported?
—Short-term impact: The market prices the breach immediately. Our model shows
cumulative breach-attributed (i.e. isolating only the part of the share price move
we can pin on the breach itself) returns are c. -2.9% within two trading days of

at one year, although the one-year point estimate (our single best-guess figure)
should be treated with caution given the width of the confidence band.
—Pre-breach price behaviour: We find no evidence of information leakage ahead
of disclosure. While the breach component for the [-10,-1] window (ten trading
days before the news broke) is +0.04%, the corresponding t-stat value of 0.03 falls
notably below the usual bar for significance (c.2). As such, we conclude the market

had not begun reacting before the announcement.
Event study methodology
—Sample: 47 publicly reported cybersecurity incidents across the US and Europe
over 2015-2025, measured against each company’s regional sector benchmark.
Requirement of 5 years of stock returns pre-attack and 1 year of post-attack.
—Day 0: the day an incident was first publicly reported or disclosed.
—Baseline: We use five years of pre-incident share-price performance to
estimate: (i) Beta (β): how sensitive a company’s returns are to movements in its

sector and (ii) Alpha (α): the extent to which the company typically outperforms
or underperforms its sector after adjusting for that sensitivity.
—Attribution: post-incident returns are split into sector-relative returns, alpha
(α) and breach (abnormal returns attributed to the disclosure).
—95% confidence interval: Range within which we can say with 95% confidence
that the real share price impact lies, based on our sample data.
Total return attribution

95% Confidence Interval Breach impact negative at 95% confidence Cumulative impact of cyberincident
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
* indicates trading windows where breach impact is significant at the 95% confidence level

### Page 14

Marks & Spencer (Reported Apr 2025)
What happened?
A breach via an attack on a connected 3rd
party forced the suspension of all online
orders and led to empty store shelves.
What did it cost?
~£136mn of direct costs related to the
attack, estimated group operating profit
impact of around £300mn for the FY. M&S
also said they had received £100mn via
cyber insurance.
Coupang (Reported Nov 2025)
What happened?
A data breach exposed the records of
more than 30mn users with a former

employee reportedly behind the attack.
What did it cost?
The firm was fined more than ~625bn won
($409mn) (2/3rd directly attributable to
data leak), and also announced a $1.2bn
voucher compensation scheme.
UnitedHealth (Reported Feb 2024)
What happened?
Hackers reportedly used stolen credentials
to access a subsidiary’s (Change
Healthcare) systems, stealing data and
deploying ransomware.
What did it cost?
Total costs exceeded $3bn, with the CEO
summoned to testify before Congress,

where he confirmed that a $22mn ransom
was paid.
Equifax (Reported Sep 2017)
What happened?
A data breach saw hackers expose
sensitive customer information, including
145.5mn Social Security numbers and
more than 200k credit card numbers.
Attackers reportedly moved inside Equifax
systems for 76 days before being detected.
What did it cost?
Settlements, fines and legal costs
exceeded $1bn, while the firm also spent
more than $1.5bn on tech upgrades

following the incident.

Notable cyber attacks: how they happened, estimated costs and share price reaction, cont.
Nike (Reported Jan 2026)
What happened?
A cybercrime group leaked what it claimed
was 1.4TB of internal Nike data (appearing
to cover metrics such as product
specifications and pricing), following expiry
of a ransom deadline.
What did it cost?
While Nike did confirm it was investigating
the incident, it did not report any
operational disruption. Underperformance

shown in the chart does not appear to be
related to the breach.
T-Mobile (Reported Aug 2021)
What happened?
An attacker reportedly targeted an
exposed router as an entry point, before
using brute force attacks to gain further
access to T-Mobile systems.
What did it cost?
In 2022 the firm agreed to pay $500mn to
settle a class-action lawsuit ($350mn
payment to class members  plus $150mn
on security spend) but has since suffered
further data breaches, contributing to an

additional $31.5mn regulatory settlement.
Coinbase (Reported May 2025)
What happened?
Attackers bribed at least one support
agent at a connected 3rd party to access
Coinbase support systems and steal
customer data, then tricked users into
sending crypto.
What did it cost?
Coinbase refused to pay the $20mn
ransom demanded by the attackers to
keep the attack quiet. Instead they
reimbursed affected users and cited costs
of up to $400mn. The stock impact was

short-lived, though MSCI applied an ESG
score deduction.
Jaguar Land Rover (Reported Sep 2025)
What happened?
Production was shut down for five weeks
due to what was deemed a ransomware
attack; though no ransom was asked for
(later reporting suggested potential Russia
links were being investigated).
What did it cost?
JLR posted £485mn Q2’ FY26 loss (vs.
£398mn profit in Q2’ FY25), with an
additional £196mn in “cyber-related costs”.
These impacts filtered through to the

share price of JLR’s Parent (Tata Motors),
while UK economic impacts were
estimated at £1.9bn.

### Page 16

16
Targets go beyond tech and can also impact critical infrastructure
MGM Resorts (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
A vishing call to the IT help desk was reportedly used to reset credentials, after which
ransomware took down hotel systems, bookings and casino floor operations for several
days.
What did it cost?
MGM declined to pay a ransom. Around $100mn hit to third-quarter 2023 results plus
roughly ~$10mn of additional one-time expenses related to the incident.

Caesars Entertainment (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
The same social-engineering group as in the MGM incident targeted an outsourced IT
support vendor, gaining access to the loyalty programme database and member data.
What did it cost?
Caesars paid roughly $15mn of a reported $30mn demand and kept customer-facing
operations running.
NHS / Synnovis (Reported Jun 2024)
What happened?
Ransomware against pathology provider Synnovis significantly reduced its capacity to

process tests leading to delays / cancellations of over 11,000 appointments.
What did it cost?
Estimated 2024 direct operational financial impact of around GBP 32mn for Synnovis,
several times the profit reported in the prior year, plus wider NHS disruption costs.
Colonial Pipeline (Reported May 2021)
What happened?
A compromised VPN credential without multi-factor authentication allowed attackers to
access the network where they subsequently deployed ransomware. The operator shut the

pipeline down, triggering fuel shortages (c. 70% of gas stations in some states ran out of
fuel) across the US East Coast.
What did it cost?
A $4.4mn ransom was paid, of which about $2.3mn was later recovered by the US
authorities.
Bitget (Reported Sep 2026)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a vulnerability in a 3rd party security product to steal internal network
credentials, forge withdrawal instructions, bypass risk controls and ultimately steal crypto.

What did it cost?
An estimated $388mn worth of funds was transferred out by the attackers. While the firm
reimbursed affected users via its User Protection Fund, there were significant outflows
following the incident as users moved to take funds off the platform.
Progress Software (Reported May 2023)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a zero-day SQL injection vulnerability in Progress Software’s MOVEit file
transfer system. This was then used by attackers to steal data from firms running the

Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026

Cyber breaches can impact consumer behaviour
Roughly 4 in 10 consumers we surveyed say they would stop buying from a company they shop with after a known breach or attack.
Source: dbDataInsights monthly consumer survey, Deutsche Bank Research. EU Average includes France, Germany, Italy and Spain.
How will customers respond to a data breach once it occurs?
dbDataInsights survey of consumer responses, % of respondents
How will customers react once the breach is resolved?

Consumer responses among those who would either stop buying or were unsure, % of respondents
Consumer surveys suggest that cyber breaches can undermine customer
loyalty.  Results of our dbDataInsights Consumer survey show the potential
mindset of a consumer after a cyber breach occurs:
—Across the US, UK and EU average, 39–41% say they would stop buying, while only
27–28% would continue. Around a third are unsure.
—Among those who would stop buying or are unsure, only 18–22% say they would

return as normal. Another 30–35% would buy selected products only, 17–20%
would not return, and 28–29% remain unsure. Note that this implies a range of
13-15% believe they would not shop at the store again after the breach is
resolved.
Admittedly, these stated intentions may overstate actual switching, particularly for
essential purchases where alternatives are limited. Nevertheless, the findings
suggest that cyber breaches that put customer privacy at risk can influence

purchasing decisions, especially if they have alternatives.

dbDataInsights Consumer Survey Methodology
—Our proprietary survey conducted in 2026 (January – September) asked

consumers how they would respond if a company they shopped at regularly
was involved in a data breach or cyberattack, and how they would respond
once the disruption was resolved. This monthly survey covers households and
consumers in Germany, France, Italy, Spain, the United Kingdom, and the
United States.
—For each country referenced above, a nationally representative sample of more
than 500 households is surveyed each month (over 600 households in the US).

Collectively, this meant over the course of the survey (January – September
2026), we surveyed almost 30,000 households.

### Page 18

18
Material cyber incidents have contributed to negative credit outlooks
The infrastructure exists for cyberattacks to have a greater impact on credit quality should cyber disruption continue to accelerate
Source: S&P, Moody’s, Fitch, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Credit rating agencies consider both pre-breach resilience frameworks and
post-breach financial impacts. Agencies assess pre-breach cyber preparedness
through a lens of mitigating risk, while post-breach credit impacts centre on

financial capacity to withstand the resulting shock (considers scale and length of
the disruption); highly leveraged issuers with limited liquidity are seen as more
vulnerable. Negative outlook changes at JLR and Equifax illustrate the potential
credit impact. Fitch generally considers permanent reductions in cash generation
more credit-significant than temporary cash-flow delays.
—Ratings agencies are moving towards continuous monitoring. In 2021,

Moody’s invested $250mn in BitSight, which produces daily security ratings.
Moody’s has since built BitSight’s ratings into its credit tools, including Credit
Catalyst and EDF-X, for uses such as underwriting and ongoing monitoring of
borrowers and suppliers. This means lenders can see a company’s exposed
vulnerabilities as a potential indicator of corporate cyber risk.
S&P Global:
“The increasing frequency of cyberattacks and the potential for rapid deterioration in credit

profiles after an attack are risk factors relevant for our rating assessments.”
Moody’s:
“Cyberattacks can undermine the creditworthiness of companies, organizations and
governments, across all sectors and geographies, hitting revenues, increasing costs, and eroding
customer trust.”
Fitch Ratings:
“Although cyber events cannot be predicted with great accuracy, the associated risk can be
managed through proactive strategies, technology, policies, and vigilance.”

“The chief executive officer and board of directors hold ultimate responsibility for managing
cyber risk.”
Example rating actions where cyber was a named factor
Outlook changes due to operational disruption, financial cost and reporting delays

it had done so and followed the 2017 attack on

Cyber governance & breaches can impact ESG scores
Sectors that hold the most personal data, and monetise it most directly, carry the greatest risk weighting in mainstream ESG scores
Source: MSCI ESG Ratings Methodology, Morningstar Sustainalytics ESG Risk Ratings, SASB Materiality Map, Deutsche Bank Resear ch.
Cybersecurity is generally seen as both a social and governance consideration
MSCI and Morningstar Sustainalytics both assess data privacy and security through management of the

risk, exposure to it and the controversies that follow a breach. Weak governance weighs on the score,
and a breach can cut it directly – more severe controversies draw larger deductions that persist for
longer.
Materiality varies sharply by sector. SASB flags data security as material across most consumer,
financial, healthcare and technology industries, with customer privacy concentrated in the most data-
intensive ones (see right).

### Page 20

IV. Investment, risk and opportunity

### Page 21

21
Europe’s dependency on US tech is firmly in the spotlight
Access to frontier AI models is now a security question; the funding gap for Europe will be hard to close
Source: Tikehau Capital – Tikehau Capital Barometer of European Investment in Cybersecurity – 7th edition 2026, Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Growing cyber risk creates increased dependency on US cyber tech. Growing
concern over the cyber capabilities of frontier AI models has made ensuring

access to such technology a national security priority. US dominance over the AI
tech chain (e.g. chips, model development, etc), its willingness to impose AI-
related controls / restrictions, and previous pressure against Europe over tech
regulations has amplified concerns that dependence on US tech represents a
strategic threat to European sovereignty.
—Funding is the structural challenge. US cybersecurity start-ups attract almost 6x
the funding of European peers, and US-domiciled firms dominate the major

indices – 89% of the Nasdaq CTA Cybersecurity index and 93% of Global X
Cybersecurity ETF (which seeks to track the performance of Indxx Cybersecurity
Index) by weight. Europe has also seen home-grown firms such as Darktrace
acquired by US companies, though this reflects broader market trends rather
than anything cyber-specific. Ultimately, the US has a greater exit strategy for
start-ups as well.
—Boosting homegrown solutions a focus, but also a risk. EU policymakers are

seeking to address the dependency; e.g. the EU’s Cybersecurity and AI Action Plan
includes “scaling up Europe’s AI capabilities for cybersecurity” as one of its three
objectives. However, there are also risks associated with this endeavour; as one
dbDataInsights survey participant put it, “for most European organisations, the
bigger near-term danger would be moving too fast to less capable providers.”
—The wider relationship adds to the exposure. A more transactional

international environment, alongside Europe’s dependency on the US for energy
supplies and defence support, compounds the risk. Recent US-EU diesel
negotiations serve as a pertinent example of this, whereby the US reportedly
considered a diesel export ban to compel a release of European diesel reserves.

### Page 22

22
Cybersecurity as an opportunity
Tailwinds include demand related to keeping operations secure with AI acceleration and consolidation tailwinds
Source: dbDataInsights, Deutsche Bank Research. Investors, defined as those who invest in stocks/ shares (either individually or through a broker/advisor) on a regular basis , were asked whether they perceived cybersecurity / data privacy as a risk or an opportunity for investors in 2026.
Broadly, DB’s company research view is that cybersecurity becomes even

more important in the AI era. Whereas cybersecurity has historically been viewed
as a blocker/drag on innovation and operational speed, it is now viewed as a
strategic enabler for secure AI adoption.
In particular, network security may see renewed growth after being viewed as a
more mature category. Rising AI usage should drive additional compute and data-
centre capacity, expanding the infrastructure requiring segmentation and
protection. Agentic workloads may also generate lower-throughput but more

frequent and persistent interactions across models, tools and data sources,
increasing the volume and complexity of network-level inspection.
Vendor consolidation has been a structural trend in cyber for some time, though not
necessarily linear. That said, the current environment may accelerate the
consolidation tailwinds as customers look to close security gaps that may arise from
a fragmented tool stack.
See further commentary from DB’s company research team here.

Our March 2026 “personal investor” survey showed a more positive view
among “ESG” investors. 58% in the US and 52% in the UK saw cybersecurity/data
privacy as an opportunity. Among all investors, US respondents favoured
opportunity over risk (46% vs 30%). UK views were more balanced (38% vs 40%).

### Page 24

24
Appendix A - Attack types
Top Attack types What it is How AI is helping attackers
Malware Software built to damage, disrupt or gain unauthorised access AI writes harmful software faster. Google has found malware that rewrites its own code while running to avoid detection, and criminal marketplaces selling AI malware-building tools.
Phishing Deceptive messages that trick people into giving up sensitive information Where AI’s effect is clearest: fluent, personalised lures without the old spelling and phrasing giveaways. Google says state-backed groups use AI to mass-produce them, and AI can fake voices and video.

Denial of service (DoS) Flooding a system so it cannot serve legitimate users Little reported evidence so far. AI can help find weak points and automate traffic floods, but the attack itself stays fairly simple.
Distributed denial of service
(DDoS)
The same attack launched from many
compromised devices at once
AI can accelerate the development and automation of attack tooling, while large-scale
botnets provide the distributed capacity behind hyper-volumetric DDoS attacks.

Man-in-the-middle (MitM) Intercepting or altering traffic between two parties Mainly indirect. AI-written phishing pages and deepfake voices make it easier to trick people into connecting through an attacker or approving fake sign-ins.
SQL injection Malicious SQL queries injected to manipulate backend database operations AI tools scan sites and spot flaws in database code faster, so less-skilled attackers can find weaknesses that once needed real expertise.

Cross-site scripting (XSS) Scripts injected into web pages to hijack user sessions As with SQL injection: AI speeds up finding vulnerable pages and writing the malicious code.
Zero-day exploits Attacking a software flaw before a patch is released A major new development. In May 2026 Google reported the first zero-day it believes was developed with AI. Its September 2026 report says the bigger effect is attackers misusing known flaws faster, before fixes land.

Advanced persistent threats
(APTs)
An intruder staying hidden in a network for long-
term
Google reports state-backed groups using AI across the whole attack, from target
research and lures to tooling and sifting stolen data.
Ransomware Encrypting data or locking device and demanding payment to release it NCSC expects AI to add to the global ransomware threat by lowering the skill needed to break in. AI also helps criminals find the most extortable data.

IoT exploitation Abusing weakly secured connected devices for entry AI can scan the internet for unpatched cameras, routers and storage devices and identify which flaws they carry.
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, National Cyber Security Centre, Google Threat Intelligence Group, Cloudflare, FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3)

### Page 25

Appendix B - European vs US cyber regulation
Europe US Federal
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Regulation Key requirements
NIS2 Directive In force 2023, transposition deadline Oct-24. Covers 18
sectors (up from seven) and creates an EU vulnerability
database. In-scope firms must manage cyber and
supply-chain risk and notify authorities of significant
incidents. Management bodies must approve and
oversee cyber risk measures, with potential liability for

infringements.
Cybersecurity Act In force 2019. Establishes an EU-wide certification
framework for ICT products, services, processes and
managed security services, and made the EU
cybersecurity body ENISA permanent.
Cyber Resilience Act In force 2024, main obligations from 2027.
Manufacturers and retailers of products with digital
elements must deliver security by design, a defined
support period and security fixes within it, plus
mandatory vulnerability management and disclosure.

Digital Operational
Resilience Act (DORA)
In force 2023, applies from 2025. Financial-services
specific, covering own-firm and third-party risk. Requires
an ICT business continuity policy, regular resilience
testing and disclosure of major incidents, and gives
regulators oversight of critical ICT providers.
Cyber Solidarity Act In force 2025. Creates a European Cybersecurity Alert
System to detect threats and share cross-border
warnings, plus a Cyber Emergency Mechanism to boost

preparedness and response.
Regulation Key requirements
White House Accord on
Super Intelligence
(2026)
Emphasises self-regulation to ensure AI safety, with a call for
external evaluations and the possibility of formal regulation in
future. Not legally binding; signed by President Trump and
leading AI companies.
Executive Order on
Cybersecurity (2025)
Highlights risk from foreign actors and orders further action
on defending digital infrastructure, securing the most vital

and governance.
CIRCIA (passed 2022,
not yet in effect)
In-scope entities, focused on critical infrastructure, must
report covered incidents to CISA within 72 hours and
ransomware payments within 24 hours of payment.
Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act
(amended 2024)
Financial institutions must explain information-sharing
practices and safeguard customer data, running a security
programme covering risk assessment, service-provider
oversight and incident response.

Federal Information
Security Modernization
Act (2014)
Applies to federal agencies and contractors: system security
plans, continuous monitoring, incident response capability
and mandatory incident reporting.
Federal Acquisition
Regulation (cyber
provisions 2016)
Governs federal procurement, setting baseline cyber
requirements for suppliers so cyber risk is built into
government contracting. Agencies may impose stricter
provisions.

Document 414: What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-10-11
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it?
A closer look at the impact of China’s currency on China’s export growth.
Sean Yong/Reuters
UPDATED
October 11, 2026 12:00 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
There is an argument—made in the recent Geneva
Report and by various former IMF officials—that
China’s current export surge is the simple
consequence of the housing downturn and the
resulting weakness in Chinese internal demand.*
The weakness in the RMB in this story is entirely
incidental to the increase in exports, which is, more or
less, a mechanical response to weak internal demand.

Cannot sell excavators at home? Export them.
Built more modern car capacity than your internal
market supports? Export that too…
The fall in the nominal RMB over the course of 2022
and the first half of 2023 in nominal and read terms
was simply a function of domestic macroeconomic
weakness, and a growing rate differential. It wasn’t an
active policy choice by China’s central bank.

### Page 3

3
Now, some might note that China’s currency doesn’t
always move with rate differentials. The RMB didn’t
consistently get pulled up when Chinese rates were
higher than U.S. rates (2002-2020 generally
speaking), and while it did fall when rate differentials
moved against the RMB in 2022 and 2023, it then
stabilized and has even appreciated modestly since
the middle of last year. That appreciation hasn’t been
big enough to correct the deep undervaluation of the

RMB, but it came at a time when the PBOC policy
rate (the reverse repo rate) was well below the U.S.
policy rate.
China doesn’t have an open financial account. As a
result, equilibrium in the foreign exchange market is
thus driven far more by exporter conversion and
current account flows than by rate differentials and
financial flows.** That, together with China’s ongoing
management of the exchange rate and a trade surplus
that can generate significant inflows, means that the

### Page 4

4
value of the RMB is much more of a policy variable
under the independent control of the Chinese
authorities than a simple byproduct of monetary and
fiscal policies.
The modern IMF is reluctant to accept this
conclusion. Its surveillance has generally ignored
China’s currency management (even if that
management is relatively obvious; see the data on FX
settlement) and its recent comprehensive assessment
of China’s economy completely ignored the balance of

payments and exchange rate management.
But it is pretty obvious to the market, even if the
PBOC, rather absurdly, insists that “market forces
play a decisive role in determining the RMB
exchange rate.”
I doubt the PBOC can win this argument. I agree
with Mark Sobel:
“The renminbi is still heavily managed and the
authorities have acted to slow appreciation. The paper
states that the PBoC phased out ‘regular’ foreign
exchange intervention in 2017. But it has many

means at its disposal to achieve the same effect
through extensive capital controls, administrative
guidance, signalling at the fix and deploying state-
owned commercial banks. These means are heavily
used. Thus, the word ‘regular’ must be taken with a
pinch of salt. The comparison to Japan, which

### Page 5

5
intervenes occasionally, is spurious – the yen largely
floats freely in integrated global markets. The same
cannot be said for the renminbi.”
In reality, the PBOC’s daily fix now plays the decisive
role in the currency’s path, and the market is often a
bet on how the PBOC’s management of the yuan will
evolve.
That is clear from the lack of volatility in the yuan’s
rate against the dollar. The yuan’s move down against
the dollar in 2022 was a controlled 10 percent move,

and the appreciation over the last 16 months has been
even more controlled crawl.
And the market is clearly massaged with substantial
help from the state banks. In fact that the pattern of
changes in the monthly FX settlement data looks
exactly like the pattern of intervention of a central
bank managing its currency around a tight band (the

### Page 6

6
state banks in practice buy FX to keep the CNY from
testing the strong side of the band, so there is an
asymmetry in how the CNY is managed)
None of this would matter much if China’s economy
was small. But that obviously isn’t the case. Nor would
this matter much if China’s exports didn’t respond to
changes in the RMB’s real (and nominal) value. But
that is also not the case.
There is abundant evidence that China’s exports do
respond to moves in the currency. Periods of RMB

weakness have consistently been associated with
periods of especially rapid Chinese export growth.

### Page 7

7
Chancellor Merz and President Macron got this
right, even if macroeconomic issues haven’t yet
figured prominently in the EU’s dialogue with
China.*** As they note in their open letter to the
European Commission:
“An appreciation of undervalued currencies towards a value
more in line with fundamentals would help support the
rebalancing of trade accounts with the EU in the short and
medium term … European policy discussions focused on
forging a common understanding of these exchange rate issues

and their interaction with global imbalances would be useful.”
The best evidence that exports responded to changes
in the currency, and not simply to changes in the path
of China’s internal demand, is the acceleration in
China’s export growth after the RMB’s depreciation
over the course of 2022.
This is the killer chart.

### Page 8

8
Now, you might ask: what of the acceleration in
export growth in 2020 and 2021? That was pretty
clearly tied to the global increase in goods demand
that came with the pandemic and work from home
requirements. It was expected to fade when the world
economy normalized. But it didn’t. By 2023 Chinese
export growth again topped global trade growth.
The general expectation that the rise in China’s goods
surplus during Covid would prove to be “transitory”

### Page 9

9
heavily influenced the IMF’s work at the time: see the
IMF’s external sector reports of 2023 and 2024.
I would argue that neglecting the exchange rate was
the key reason the IMF’s kept forecasting, incorrectly,
that China’s current account surplus would fall (See
this blog with Shahin Vallee). The IMF certainly
didn’t warn that China’s property downturn and the
RMB’s real depreciation risked a second China shock
in its 2022, 2023, or 2024 surveillance reports.

Concretely, the weaker RMB appears to have
contributed to a very large outperformance of China’s
exports relative to global trade. See the following
chart
That incidentally also happened after the RMB
followed the dollar down in 2003.
In both periods, there were significant structural
shifts. The WTO accession in 2002, and the success

### Page 10

10
of Chinese industrial policies in the “green” industrial
sectors and autos in 2022. In both cses exports were
turbocharged when structural shifts that should have
resulted in a stronger currency were instead amplified
by currency weakness.
No matter. Paul Krugman has noted that a country
that is converging technologically with its trade
partners should see a trend appreciation in its
currency. China got the technological convergence,
but not the real appreciation—at least not until this

year. And the move since the middle of 2025 has
corrected the depreciation of the RMB that occurred
when the RMB followed the dollar down on
liberation day, but not the structural overvaluation.
Now, there is an argument that exchange rate
adjustment is too hot an issue to make it a priority.
The PBOC isn’t interested it seems.
And some believe that in the interest of comity and
good relations with China, the IMF should mirror
the PBOC’s views. After all a stronger RMB would

slow China’s exports and thus slow China’s economy
at a time when China needs export growth to escape
deflation and generate employment. Or so the
argument goes. Concerns about “external balance“
and the negative global spillovers from China’s rising
goods surplus should be ignored and the Fund’s sole
focus should be on policies that lead to internal

### Page 11

11
balance. That provides one rationale for the Fund’s
ongoing decision to essentially ignore the contentious
issue of the PBOC’s ongoing exchange rate
management.
That argument, however, assumes that China’s
economy is small and thus China’s external surplus
can safely be ignored because it isn’t a drag on the
growth of the IMF’s other members.
That clearly isn’t true.
And it isn’t clear that China is more interested in
having a negotiation over its level of basic pension

benefits or the size of its fiscal deficit (where the
IMF continues to make timid recommendations that
would not move the needle—and certainly wouldn’t
meaningfully reduce China’s external surplus) than a
discussion over its currency practices.****
Back in the world of fixed but adjustable exchange
rates, there was an expectation (not always met to be
sure) that surplus countries would allow their
currencies to strengthen to bring their economies

back into external balance, and that other policy tools
would be used to ensure internal balance.
I would argue that China’s exchange rate (against
both the dollar and the basket) has many similarities
to the old world of fixed but adjustable currencies.
True, it moves on a controlled path when it moves

### Page 12

12
between different levels rather than jumping up and
down. But the PBOC does frequently target a level,
and that targeted level does change over time.
I would further argue that steps like the recent tariff
rate quota that will limit China’s exports of hybrid
cars to Europe to around 350,000 units next year (up
from 2025, but well below the 800,000 units of
2026) aren’t going to address the underlying
imbalance on their own. They can provide a bit of a

buffer for specific sectors.
But that buffer, while necessary, won’t address the
broad-based erosion of manufacturing capacity
outside China. This erosion will persist as long as an
undervalued RMB sends the entire world a signal to
use China as its production base.
The new system of managed trade that is emerging in
autos would need to be progressively expanded to an
ever-increasing set of sectors if protection is used as a
substitute for both domestic reform in China and

exchange rate adjustment.
That is now the direction of travel, but it doesn’t
address the underlying systemic issues.
Incidentally, those who believe the path of internal
demand is critical to rebalancing and that exchange
rate moves are secondary need to explain how an
acceleration in domestic demand would, on its own,

### Page 13

13
lead China’s external accounts to adjust. One
important, stylized fact about China is that domestic
demand growth is no longer feeding over to import
growth (ex-gold, that is).
Bottom line: China, which needs a 1.5 percentage
point contribution from net exports every year, is on a
collision course with its trading partners without a
currency correction that provides the basis for more
balanced trade even in the absence of trade
restrictions.
And there isn’t a realistic path towards balance that

doesn’t involve a stronger RMB; there aren’t, as Paul
Krugman once put it, immaculate transfers.
* That downturn shows up in domestic deflationary
pressure, and weak job growth/high rates of (at least
youth) unemployment. China, in other words, isn’t in
“internal balance.” I really liked the report from

### Page 14

14
Kamakshya Trivedi and Hui Shan of Goldman Sachs
arguing China needs two tools to hit two targets
(internal and external balance).
** To be sure, rate differentials matter for exporter
conversion—but so does the expected movement of
the RMB. The market understands this better than
the Fund. The Chen and Suwanapruti (Goldman
Sachs) note uses a framework similar to mine to
describe the CNY’s moves in Q3:
“In our view, sizeable FX inflows have continued to support

RMB appreciation despite unfavorable interest rate
differentials, as strong export receipts and increased exporter
FX conversions have outweighed portfolio outflows. Our
estimated FX conversion ratio rose to 63% in August,
compared with around 45% in normal years, likely reflecting a
shift in market expectations from RMB depreciation to
appreciation.”
*** Š ef č ovi č  has jurisdiction over trade policy
(narrowly defined), but not broader macroeconomic

policies. Europe’s institutional architecture for
exchange rate coordination is not well-developed.
**** The current account surplus is no longer masked
to the same extent as in 2023 and 2024, as SAFE has
fiddled with its BoP methodology once again (per the
Federal Reserve) and the BoP goods surplus is no
longer significantly below the customs goods surplus.
Of course, there are still problems in the data—the
deficit in the investment income account most

Will earnings resolve 
the bond-equity split?

 Bond and equity market volatility has 
sharply diverged. Elevated bond 
volatility and political risks in the US 
and Europe warrant near-term caution, 
prompting us to lock in tactical gains in 
semiconductors and Taiwan equities. 
 Our quantitative stock-bond model 
turned negative for the first time since 
March 2025, signalling near-term equity 
volatility. We stay invested in a 
diversified allocation slightly overweight 
equities and prefer to navigate the 
upcoming results and macro events 
before adding back high-beta exposure 
within equities. 
 Consensus estimates point to a 31% 
y/y rise in S&P500 Q3 earnings, driven 
by technology-sector earnings growth 
of 67%. Earnings have beaten lofty 
estimates in H1 as the AI build-out 
exceeds expectations. This favours 
tech-led equities in the medium term.

risks cloud the EUR outlook, but 
regional bank equities remain attractive

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 9 October 2026

Charts of the week: Diverging volatility

Strong earnings should resolve the bond and equity market divergence; French political risks remain largely contained  
S&P500 volatility index (VIX); US bond volatility index (MOVE)*   10-year government bond yield spreads over German bonds

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered; *measures US government bond volatility

Editorial 
Will earnings resolve the bond-equity split?  
Strategy summary:  Bond and equity market volatility has 
sharply diverged. Elevated bond volatility and political risks in 
the US and Europe warrant near-term caution, prompting us to 
lock in tactical gains in semiconductors and Taiwan equities. 
Our quantitative stock-bond model turned negative for the first 
time since March 2025, signalling near-term equity volatility . 
We stay invested in a diversified allocation  slightly overweight 
equities and prefer to navigate the upcoming results and macro 
events before adding high-beta exposure within equities. 
Consensus estimates point to a c. 31% y/y rise in S&P500 Q3 
earnings, driven by technology -sector earnings growth of c. 
67%. Earnings have beaten lofty estimates in H1 as the AI 
build-out exceeds expectations. This favours tech -led equities 
in the medium term. In Europe, French political and fiscal risks 
cloud the EUR outlook, but regional bank equities remain 
attractive on robust profitability, capital strength and valuations. 
Divergence between equities and bonds.  Equity markets 
signal calm, with the S&P 500 index near record highs and its 
volatility index (VIX) low at 15, while the US government bond 
volatility index (MOVE) is close to its highest since March. Bond 
stress reflects two-decade-high long yields amid strong growth, 
oil close to USD 100/bbl fuelling near -term inflation, fiscal 
uncertainty and crowded investor short positioning vulnerable 
to abrupt unwinds. High bond volatility could spill into equities. 
Our quantitative stock-bond model turns negative. For the 
first time since March 2025, the model score has turned 
negative, driven by deteriorating equity market breadth and 
above-average risk aversion. History shows global equity 
volatility rises most in the month after the score dips to -1. 
However, equities still outperformed bonds over the following 
1-12 months, on average, despite higher market risk. 
Taking some chips off the table; locking in gains:  Rising 
bond volatility, the negative stock -bond model signal, weak 
equity breadth and approaching US mid-term elections warrant 
near-term caution. US small -caps remain about 9% below 
August’s all-time highs even as large-caps reach new records. 
The macro uncertainty prompted us to lock in gains in 
semiconductor and Taiwan equity opportunistic ideas. 
Strong Q3 earnings season likely to overcome near -term 
challenges. Strong US and Asian earnings should ultimately 
resolve the equity -bond divergence in favour of stocks. 
Consensus S&P 500 Q3 earnings growth has risen to 30 .6% 
y/y, while technology is tracking 66.5%, driven by the AI 
infrastructure buildout. AI investment plans, monetisation and 
funding needs are key metrics to watch. Record memory -chip 
profits and higher long -term targets from AI leaders support 
staying long technology-led risk assets and using near -term 
volatility to scale into preferred sectors. Also, US equities have 
historically performed strongly in the 12 months after mid -term 
elections, particularly when the opposition regains control of the 
House and the Senate – as seems increasingly likely. 
Medium-term constructive on equities. Beyond Q3 earnings, 
we expect US earnings to rise 15-20% next year. Almost half of 
the estimated USD 420 per share of S&P500 earnings in 2027 
should come from tech-related sectors: information technology, 
communication services and consumer discretionary. We also 
favour US, Europe and Japan financial sectors as solid growth 
and higher rates lift lending income, while the AI buildout boosts 
advisory fees from IPOs and M&A s. However, a nother 30-
50bps rise in long-term bond yields , without earnings growth 
upgrades, could be a  headwind for equities.  Next week’s US 
inflation data for September is the next catalyst for bond yields. 
French uncertainty challenges near -term EUR view; 
continue to see opportunities in Euro area bank equities.  
Rising French political and fiscal risks have largely remained 
confined to France, with its bond yield spread versus German 
peers surging to the widest since the 2011-12 Euro area crisis. 
Nevertheless, EUR/USD has fallen around 4% since mid -
August, and our three-month forecast of 1.15 is under review. 
Tighter financial conditions and political uncertainty in France 
and Spain raises the risk of a n ECB rate hold. Euro area bank 
equities remain attractive on profitability, capital strength and 
valuations, subject to sovereign-spread and funding-cost risks.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya and Sundeep Gantori

### Page 4

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 9 October 2026

Top client questions 
  What are the market implications of France's political and 
fiscal uncertainty?   
Our view: France’s political and fiscal uncertainty remain near-
term headwinds for European assets. We are placing our three-
month EUR/USD forecast (1.15) under review for a potential 
downward adjustment, while retaining our 12-month forecast at 
1.18 as broader Euro  area spillovers remain limited. Our base 
case remains one more ECB hike in December followed by a 
prolonged pause, although tighter financial conditions increase 
the risk of a delay. For equities, we retain Euro area banks as 
an Opportunistic idea, supported by strong profitability, capital 
buffers and reasonable valuations. For bank bonds, we prefer a 
subordinated capital structure to senior debt. 
Rationale: France’s 10-year government bond yield has risen amid 
renewed political and fiscal concerns. This has tightened financial 
conditions and added to the near-term EUR risk premium. Together 
with elevated US yields, this raises downside risks to our three-
month EUR/USD view. However, the stress has not yet developed 
into broad Euro area contagion, and recent expectations of progress 
on France’s 2027 budget have provided some relief. Broader Euro  
area growth and labour  market fundamentals also remain relatively 
resilient, supporting our unchanged 12-month EUR/USD view. 
For the ECB, higher sovereign yields are already delivering some 
monetary tightening. Markets have consequently pared expectations 
of additional ECB hikes. We continue to expect one more hike in 
December, given elevated near -term inflation, but persistent fiscal 
or political stress in France and Spain could delay further tightening. 
For Euro area banks, wider sovereign spreads  are likely to  raise 
funding costs and pressure bond portfolios, while fewer ECB hikes 
would moderate further gains in net interest income. Nevertheless, 
profitability remains strong, capital buffers are healthy and price-
to-book valuations remain reasonable, with returns on equity also 
improving. We, therefore, maintain Euro area banks as an 
Opportunistic idea , while monitoring sovereign spreads, funding 
costs and any deterioration in loan-loss provisions.  
On the bond side, European bank junior subordinated bond prices 
fell by an average of 3-5 cash points before rebounding, with French 
bank subordinated bonds underperforming those of other European 
peers. As fundamentals remain strong, we view pullbacks as 
opportunities to add and maintain our preference for a subordinated 
capital structure over senior debt for European bank bonds. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
— Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist  
 Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 9 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is the likelihood of elevated long -end government 
bond yields evolving into a systemic risk for the financial sector 
and public finances? 
Our view: We believe s ystemic risk remains a tail risk rather 
than a base case in the near term. The probability would rise 
materially if rollover stress, foreign demand withdrawal and 
private credit deterioration occur in combination. 
Rationale: Higher bond yields worsen fiscal deficits and drive higher 
issuance requirements, exerting further upward pressure on yields. 
The resulting tighter financial conditions weigh on economic growth. 
This feedback loop is most acute in Developed Markets (DMs) where 
debt burdens are already elevated. For instance, projected 2026 
debt-to-GDP ratios stand at 204% for Japan, 138% for Italy, 126% 
for the US, 118% for France and 104% for the UK. Countries with 
shorter average debt maturities face the sharpest near-term 
repricing risk  as maturing obligations are refinanced at current 
rates. Meanwhile, evidence suggests that  a positive equity-bond 
correlation increases the sensitivity of yields to bond supply.  
Taking the US as an example, there has been no buyers’ strike on 
US government bonds yet , though foreign demand has softened, 
narrowing the margin of safety. The US government has shifted 
towards shorter-dated issuance to manage funding costs (reducing 
near-term expenses, but concentrating rollover risk). Economic 
resilience remains evident, underpinned by corporate confidence 
in AI -driven investment . The key question is whether this 
expansion can persist long enough to stabilise the fiscal trajectory. 
Within the financial sector, corporate balance sheets  remain 
strong, while High-Yield (HY) bond and leveraged loan default rates 
remain contained, though this reflects the lag between rate rises and 
credit deterioration, not immunity. Following the failure of the Silicon 
Valley Bank, liquidity discipline among banks has improved, but 
duration mismatches persist.  
In private credit, default rates are trending upward while the asset 
class has come under continued scrutiny , with negative headlines 
and a rise in redemptions trigger ing fund gating  at some  private 
credit managers since Q 1 2026. Hence, continued monitoring of 
default rates, redemptions and potential stress events in private 
credit remains essential . That said, higher rates could buoy 
medium-term returns and distribution yields in the asset class , 
as the majority of direct lending loans have a floating-rate structure. 
—  Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What is your outlook for the US Q3 2026 earnings season, 
especially for the banking sector? 
Our view: We expect another quarter of robust US earnings 
growth to support our Overweight stance on US equities. We 
see m ajor US banks, which begin reporting next week, also 
delivering healthy results, reinforcing our Overweight view on  
the US financial sector. 
Rationale: Consensus forecasts S&P500 Q3 earnings growth  of 
30.6% y/y, up from 27.6% at the start of July. Energy is expected to 
post the strongest growth at 114.7%, supported by elevated oil 
prices amid ongoing geopolitical tensions. Information technology 
(66.5%) and communication services (49.0%) remain key 
earnings drivers, benefiting from sustained AI -related demand and 
investment. The broader outlook remains supportive, with full-year 
earnings growth projected at 35.8% in 2026 and 15.6% in 2027. 
For banks, the recent underperformance amid concerns over higher 
long-term bond yields has, in our view, improved the forward-looking 
risk-reward profile.  Over the medium term, strengthening loan 
growth and resilient credit quality should continue to support solid 
fundamentals. Capital markets activity also remains constructive, 
with industry leaders well -positioned to benefit from their strong 
investment banking and trading franchises. 
—  Jason Wong, Senior Equity Analyst 
  How could upcoming semiconductor manufacturing and 
equipment earnings reshape the AI capex outlook? 
Our view: We maintain our AI capex outlook as robust demand 
and ongoing supply constraints support a multi-year expansion 
cycle. In the near term, however, we take a more prudent stance 
on semiconductors amid an expected rise in equity volatility. 
Rationale: Recent memory industry guidance points to sustained 
investment in advanced chip manufacturing , with customer 
commitments extending through 2031. Upcoming earnings should 
provide visibility into capacity expansion plans. This supports our AI 
capex outlook, which we will monitor through the earnings season.  
That said, we expect equity volatility to rise in the near term amid 
elevated long-term yields and macroeconomic uncertainty. Recent 
media reports on AI lab revenues  have also led to doubts over 
monetisation and the ability to sustain elevated capex spending. We 
thus took profit on our Global Semiconductors (8.1% return from 9-
Sep-26 to 2-Oct-26) and MSCI Taiwan (10.0% return from 9-Jul-26 to 
2-Oct-26) Opportunistic ideas following strong performance. This does 
not reflect a deterioration in fundamentals, but rather a need to be 
prudent on high-beta exposure. We remain constructive on the AI 
theme over the long term. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist

### Page 7

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 9 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  USD/CHF has rebounded sharply over the past two 
months. What are the key factors behind the move, and do you 
see the currency pair continuing to move higher? 
Our view:  USD/CHF tested 0.84 amid strong momentum from 
the USD, but we see the pair remain ing supported near -term 
before gradually moving lower towards 0.82 in three months. 
Rationale: The Fed’s relatively high policy rate continues to support 
USD carry demand , while the Swiss National Bank maintains its 
policy rate at 0%. In the near term, however, the Swiss franc (CHF) 
could benefit from increased safe-haven demand amid mounting 
fiscal concerns in France  and political uncertainty in Spain , 
particularly if European risk premia continue to rise. Beyond these 
cyclical factors, Switzerland’s substantial net foreign asset position, 
persistent current account surpluses and low public debt burden 
continue to underpin the CHF’s structural strength . Over the 
medium term, we expect the USD’s rate advantage to gradually 
diminish as the Fed approaches the end of its tightening cycle, while 
the CHF’s defensive characteristics should remain supportive.  
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  Do you see further upside for USD/JPY after recent dovish 
comments from BoJ officials and latest bond auctions? 
Our view: We see some near-term upside risk to USD/JPY from 
elevated US  yields, but retain our medium -term constructive 
JPY view. USD/JPY remains below 159, suggesting markets are 
mindful of intervention risk as the pair approaches 160. We 
continue to expect further BoJ normalisation. 
Rationale: Recent BoJ communication appears less dovish than the 
market narrative had suggested. BoJ Governor Ueda continues to 
emphasise the need to anchor underlying inflation around 2%  
and gradually reduce monetary accommodation, supporting our view 
that the tightening cycle is not yet over. An October pause would 
reflect a more measured pace of policy normalisation  rather than a 
change in the BoJ’s policy direction. 
Recent Japanese government bond (JGB) auctions have shown 
resilient demand despite higher yields.  At the latest 10 -year JGB 
auction, the bid -to-cover ratio improved  to 3.76x from 3.29x 
previously, while the auction tail (spread between highest 
accepted yield and issued yield) narrowed to 0.2bps from 1.6bps. 
We see this as indicative of an orderly, functioning market rather than 
a dovish BoJ signal . Intervention concerns around  the 160 level 
should help limit near-term USD/JPY upside.  
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

Document 420: “DeepSeek才是真正的OpenAI”

Source PDF: all report pdf/“DeepSeek才是真正的OpenAI”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

OpenAI 的名字⾥虽然带着 “ 开放 ” ，但其核⼼模型⻓期采取闭源策略，反倒是来⾃中国的
DeepSeek 正⽤实际⾏动践⾏ “ 开放 ” ⼀词，可能是对开源⼤语⾔模型⽣态贡献最⼤的公司之
⼀。
摘要：
在美国⼈⼯智能（ AI ）企业专注价格昂贵的闭源模型之际， DeepSeek 将⾃⼰的技术 “ 家底 ” 向外
界开放，赢得海外⽹友称赞。
10 ⽉ 20 ⽇， DeepSeek 宣布开源内部⽣产级推理框架 DeepInfer 。
该框架是 DeepSeek 在⾯临美国尖端芯⽚出⼝限制下，确保⼤模型紧追美国顶尖 AI 模型的核⼼。
推理框架负责管理模型如何使⽤计算资源来处理⽤⼾请求，其设计会影响延迟、吞吐量以及运
⾏模型所需的硬件，因此是影响 AI 运营成本的重要因素。
美国彭博社 21 ⽇指出，推理技术通常被 AI 公司视为核⼼竞争⼒，较少对外公开，⽽ DeepSeek 此
次公开推理框架，让外界得以⼀窥其是如何在压低运⾏成本的同时，确保 AI 模型性能的。
即使是同⼀款 AI 模型，实际运⾏成本也可能因所采⽤的软件不同⽽存在较⼤差异。更加⾼效的软

件能够降低每次请求所需的计算资源，并有望进⼀步减少能源消耗。
DeepInfer 整合了分布式推理、投机解码等多项技术，能够让 AI ⼯作负载分配到不同硬件上，从
⽽让模型在更短时间内⽣成更多输出，并进⼀步挖掘芯⽚的性能。⽬前，该框架已兼容英伟达
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

SM100 GPU 和华为昇腾 950 NPU 。
这其中不少的底层技术已被业界⼴泛采⽤，但 DeepSeek 针对⾃⾝模型进⾏了专⻔的优化，使这
些技术能够更加⾼效地协同运⾏。
美媒称，对于 AI 托管服务商⽽⾔，更低的推理成本意味着更⼤的利润空间，并有可能进⼀步转化
为更低的 AI 服务价格。对于企业⽤⼾来说，如果选择在⾃有基础设施上运⾏ DeepSeek 模型，
DeepInfer 也可能帮助其降低算⼒成本。
不仅如此， DeepSeek 开源 DeepInfer 后，其他开发者也可以研究其中的技术⽅案，并将部分⽅
法应⽤到⾃⼰的模型推理系统中。
在 DeepSeek 宣布开源 DeepInfer 的帖⼦下⽅，美国加州⼀家专注于⼤语⾔模型与 AI 开源优化、
本地训练和推理的公司 Unsloth AI 公开感谢 DeepSeek 称，其已经借助 Dynamic GGUFs ，让
DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash-1017 能够在配备 128GB 内存的设备上实现本地运⾏。
评论区还出现了⼤量海外开发者和 AI 从业者的积极反馈。⼀名来⾃印度的社交媒体博主直接

⽤ “Real OPEN AI” （真正的开放 AI ）评价 DeepSeek，并配上⼀段调侃 OpenAI ⾸席执⾏官⼭姆 ·
奥尔特曼的视频。
欧洲 AI 从业者也⽤漫画嘲讽美国 AI 公司的闭源模型仅限特定 “ 朋友圈 ” ，与之相对的是中国公司通
过降低成本，让 AI 模型更普惠。

⼀名来⾃孟加拉国的软件开发者赞叹说， DeepSeek 可能是对开源⼤语⾔模型⽣态贡献最⼤的公
司之⼀。
当然， DeepSeek 此次发布的并不是⼀套完整的推理技术栈。其⽂档提到，要构建完整的服务，
还需要 KV 缓存系统、 Prefill/Decode 解耦，以及在数据并⾏副本之间进⾏负载均衡等功能，这些
内容并未完全包含在此次开源项⽬中。不过，⼤部分核⼼的解耦功能已经实现。
AI 基础设施初创公司 Requesty ⾸席执⾏官蒂博 · 杰古（ Thibault Jaigu ）表⽰， DeepInfer 降低了
部署 DeepSeek 模型的⻔槛，但企业仍需要具备相应的算⼒和技术能⼒。他认为， DeepSeek 实
际上是在告诉外界， “ 任何⼈都可以⾃⾏托管我们的模型，不过要实现⾼效的管理，还是有⼀些
技巧需要注意 ” 。
在⼴⼤海外⽹友看来， DeepSeek 所做的已不仅仅是发布⼀个开源推理框架，⽽是它正在把越来
越多原本属于企业内部的技术公开出来，让更多⼈能够参与进来。这与美国 AI ⾏业越来越强调闭
源模型、庞⼤的算⼒投⼊和商业护城河的路线形成鲜明对⽐。
OpenAI 的名字⾥虽然带着 “ 开放 ” ，但其核⼼模型⻓期采取闭源策略，反倒是来⾃中国的
DeepSeek 正⽤实际⾏动践⾏ “ 开放 ” ⼀词。
“ 为善与众⾏之，为巧与众能之。 ” 正如 DeepSeek 所说， “ 我们期待着在不远的未来，能够看到全
球 AI 服务的范围与质量获得前所未有的扩展与提升，变得更加公平、透明、可信赖，进⽽实现在
全社会范围内加快技术迭代、减少能源浪费。 ”
本⽂来源：⼤橘财经

熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
《東協尚未擺脫整理，美印關係改善訊號浮現》
9月MSCI東協指數下跌 2.6%，雖然全球公債殖利率
與油價走升帶來評價面壓力 ，但從股價跌幅及外資
賣超規模來看 ，本波修正仍偏向短線漲多後的技術
性調節。修正後，東協股市本益比已由高於近兩年
平均2倍標準差回落至約 1倍標準差 ，RSI亦降至40
以下，顯示市場過熱情況明顯改善 。然而在聯準會
升息及殖利率維持高檔的背景下 ，若基本面未能提
供更多上修空間 ，預期股市短期仍將維持震盪整理
格局。印度方面，9月回檔已使股價回測 3月低點，
本益比降至 19.9倍，RSI跌破30，顯示短線賣壓已

有過度反應。此外，莫迪於9月金磚峰會公開反對去
美元化，近期川普亦表態支持印歐經濟走廊 ，反映
美印經貿關係出現改善訊號 。後續可持續關注雙邊
合作進展及其對印度中長期投資影響。
市場投資展望
資產 4Q26看法 投資展望
東南亞 中性 估值及RSI已回到相對合理位置，但殖利率比原先預期更高，基本面需要看到優於預期的表現才有上調空間。
印度 正向 估值及RSI呈現超賣，近期美印雙方經貿合作釋出改善訊號，接下來可開始關注實質政策進展，及相關投資機會(投資與基礎建設概念)

《外資9月轉為小幅賣超，呼應8月轉中立看法》
8月下調東協評等至中立 ，主要考量 6至8月股市累
積漲幅已大致反映第三季經濟維持穩健成長的預期 ，
在缺乏新的催化劑下 ，市場進一步上行空間受限 。
進入9月後，美國公債殖利率與國際油價同步走升，
使市場重新評估高利率與成本壓力對企業獲利的影
響，並加大非科技類股及非科技市場的修正幅度 。
不過，相較於美國公債殖利率持續創高所帶來的壓
力，東協股市 9月僅下跌 2.6%，跌幅仍屬溫和；從
資金面來看 ，外資雖轉為淨賣超 ，但賣超規模明顯
低於第二季調整期間 ，顯示本波修正較偏向漲多後
的獲利了結與資產重新配置 ，而非反映市場對區域
基本面的明顯轉弱或出現恐慌性撤資。

《出口支撐成長敘事未變 ，但高通膨也比預期持久 》
東協及印度經濟仍展現韌性，多數國家8月出口維持
雙位數成長且高於長期平均 ，與全球製造業擴張趨
勢相符 ，其中新加坡及馬來西亞受惠 AI強勁資本支
出，8月出口成長年增率逾 5成，顯示外需仍是重要
成長支撐 。強勁的出口與製造活動不僅有助支撐企
業獲利 ，也在一定程度上抵銷高利率對消費與投資
的壓力 。另一方面 ，印尼、菲律賓等先前高通膨國
家雖已進入降溫趨勢 ，但通膨回落速度仍較緩慢 ，
使央行在降息時程上維持審慎 。整體而言 ，目前東
協與印度景氣尚未出現明顯轉弱 ，但高利率環境可
能維持較市場預期更久 ，意味短期經濟成長動能將
更仰賴外需及製造業活動的支撐。
重點摘要
• 東協及印度出口維持強勁成

長，反映全球製造業景氣仍
具韌性 ，並支撐區域經濟表
現；惟高通膨國家物價降溫
速度偏慢 ，恐使高利率環境
維持更久 ，短期成長仍須仰
賴外需支撐。

《川普表態支持印歐經濟走廊 ，重構印度樞紐角色 》
川普近期表態支持印歐經濟走廊 (IMEC)，使這項拜
登任內支持，但因加薩戰爭與區域衝突而停滯近3年
的計畫再度受到市場關注 。隨著中東地緣風險升溫 ，
紅海及荷姆茲海峽等關鍵航道不確定性提高 ，各國
對替代性能源與運輸路徑的需求同步增加 。從規劃
路線來看 ，印歐經濟走廊以印度為起點 ，串連海灣
產油國與歐洲市場 ，若計畫順利推進 ，除有助印度
取得更穩定的中東能源供給 、降低對俄羅斯石油的
依賴外 ，也將進一步鞏固其作為全球製造與供應鏈
樞紐的地位 。對印股而言 ，IMEC有望同時強化能源
安全與供應鏈地位 ，鞏固印度在全球製造的地位 ，
並為基建、工業及製造類股帶來中長期題材支撐。

重點摘要
• 川普表態支持擱置近 3年的印
歐經濟走廊重啟 ，在中東地
緣風險升溫下 ，有助印度強
化能源安全 、降低對俄油依
賴，並鞏固全球製造與供應
鏈樞紐地位。

chrischenlc@yuanta.com

3Q/4Q26 EPS 下修 5/14%，主因產能不足+ 歐洲市場待復甦 
上銀 3Q26/4Q26 營收估 81.1/81.3 億元，季增 8%/季持平，下修 5/14%，主
因公司人力產能提升不足且新招工學習曲線須時間提升。整體 2H26 將持續
受惠中國十五五規劃帶動工具機產業升級、 AI 資料中心與機器人帶動螺桿 /
線軌等自動化零組件需求成長。歐洲市場需求尚未顯著成長，(過往擴張時期
PMI 達 55-65，仍高於 9M26 52.9) ，估使公司於短期產能擴張速度較過去上
升循環為保守。毛利率估 36.0/35.3%，季增 1.8/季減 0.7 個 ppts，下修 0.1
個 ppts/維持前次預估。EPS 估達 3.1/3.0 元，下修 5/14%。 
短期受產能不足壓抑出貨動能，長期看好中/歐需求回升 
儘管短期受人力瓶頸與歐洲市場 處復甦初期而壓抑出貨動能，然長線公司將
受惠 1) 歐洲市場復甦（高毛利歐洲營收占公司營收 25–28%）; 2) 半導體設
備放量; 3) 訂單能見度拉長伴隨產業漲價挹注毛利率。考量產能提升受限與
歐洲市場復甦力道略低於預期 ，估上銀 2026/27 年營收年增 24%/16%至
301.2/348.6 億元，下修 6%/10%；毛利率 34.5%/35.0%，年增 5.7/0.5 個
ppts，下修 0.1/0.1 個 ppts；營益率 14.6%/17.7%，下修 0.2/0.1 個 ppts；估
EPS 達 10.1/13.5 元，年增 133%/34%，下修 6%/10%。 
AI 驅動產業續強，然短期待歐洲市場成長帶動第二波漲價 
本中心認為上銀短期因產線人力瓶頸壓抑產能擴充速度，且整體需求仍待歐
洲市場強勁復甦以帶動第二波產業漲價。 然公司積極布局 AI/半導體/人形機
器人等核心關鍵零組件，將持續優化產品組合。上銀歷史本益比區間為 20–
76 倍（五年均值 34 倍）；考量產能提升受阻、歐洲顯著復甦時程後移及全
球升息壓抑非 AI 資本支出，目標本益比由 40 倍調降至 36 倍，以 2026–2027
年平均 EPS 11.8 元，推得目標價 420 元，維持買進評等。 
元大觀點 
⧫  
3Q26/4Q26 EPS 估 3.1/3.0 元，下修 5/14%，主因人力產能提升不足。
中國需求維持高檔，漲價需待歐洲市場顯著成長。 
⧫  
考量產能提升受限與歐洲市場仍待復甦 ，估上銀 2026/27 年 EPS 達
10.1/13.5 元，下修 6%/10%。 
⧫  
AI 帶動自動化需求回升， 然短期待歐洲市場復甦帶動第二波漲價， 以
2026-27 年平均 EPS 與 36 倍本益比得目標價 420 元，買進評等。

機械、半導體設備、輸送搬運機器、工具機、自動化工程設備、醫療設備以及LCD

3Q26 EPS 估 3.1 元，下修 5%主因營收規模略低於預期，受限人力產能提升不足 
上銀 3Q26 營收 81.1 億元，季增 8%/年增 35%，低於本中心預期 5%，主因其人力產能提升不足且
新招工產能學習曲線須時間提升。營收季增主因來自 1H26 漲價效應顯現。整體 2H26 持續受惠中
國十五五規劃帶動工具機產業升級、 AI 資料中心與機器人帶動螺桿 /線軌等自動化零組件需求成長。
歐洲市場需求持續回升，其 PMI 以連續 8 個月維持擴張態勢且續創新高。此外，半導體自動化設備
持續出貨挹注。毛利率估 36.0%，季增 1.8 個 ppts/年增 7.4 個 ppts；營益率 16.6%，季增 2.8 個
ppts/年增 9.8 個 ppts，均下修 0.1 個 ppts，主因營收規模較預期低。EPS 估達 3.1 元，季增 28%/
年增 143%，下修 5%。

4Q26 EPS 估 3.0 元，下修 14%，推測為產能提升不足且需求須待歐洲市場復甦 
估上銀 4Q26 營收 81.3 億元，季持平，下修 14%，主因公司人力產能提升不足且新招工產能學習
曲線時間仍須提升；此外，本中心推估儘管中國市場持續受惠十五五規劃帶動工具機產業升級、 AI
資料中心與機器人帶動螺桿 /線軌等自動化零組件需求成長，然歐洲市場需求未見顯著成長， (過往
擴張時期 PMI 達 55-65，仍高於 9M26 52.9)，使公司於短期產能擴張速度較過去上升循環為保守。
毛利率估 35.3%，季減 0.7/年增 8.3 個 ppts；營益率 17.2%，季增 0.6 個 ppts/年增 12.4 個 ppts，
均維持前次預估。EPS 估達 3.0 元，季減 4%/年增 125%，下修 14%。

短期受產能不足壓抑出貨動能，長期看好中/美 AI 需求爆發與歐洲市場復甦 
本中心認為整體工具機與自動化產業成長將延續至 2027 年，主要受惠 1、中國工業與自動化需求
受財政擴張政策與十五五規劃刺激挹注，同時 AI 需求帶動包含半導體移載設備、AI 伺服器帶動高
階工具機及 AI 機器人需求回升。2、歐洲市場 PMI 持續回升，以維持擴張態勢達 8 個月，估隨非車
用領域如國防、醫療、半導體需求顯著成長，其歐洲訂單能見度與需求將持續攀升。上銀為全球線
軌/螺桿領導廠商， 儘管短期受限於產線人力招募不如預期，致使產能擴張進度相對緩慢，壓抑短
線出貨動能，然長期公司將受惠 1、中國與歐洲市場復甦。(尤其歐洲市場毛利率為公司最高，占比
約 25-28%)。2、半導體自動化設備放量。 3、線軌/螺桿訂單能見度提升、產業漲價帶動毛利率下 ，
本中心預估上銀 2026/27 年營收年增 24%/16%至 301.2/348.6 億元，下修 6%/10%，下修主因產能
提升限制及需待歐洲市場顯著成長。毛利率 34.5%/35.0%，年增 5.7/0.5 個 ppts，下修 0.1/0.1 個
ppts。營益率估 14.6/17.7%，下修 0.2/0.1 個 ppts。估 2026/27 年 EPS 達 10.1/13.5 元，年增
133%/34%，下修 6%/10%。

### Page 8

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：電子設備 2026 年 10 月 15 日 Hiwin 第8 頁，共 18頁  
產業概況  
台灣線性元件在世界供應鏈擔任重要角色 
一個完整的線性傳動元件廠有四項必要元件： 線性滑軌、滾珠螺桿、滾珠花鍵、單軸機器人。滑軌
主要功用是移動物件，物件會固定在滑座上，然滑座無法自行移動，因此需要馬達結合滾珠螺桿，
透過圓周轉換成直線運動。若想將物件在半空中移動，則必須將線性滑軌替換為滾珠花鍵，係因滾
珠花鍵是圓柱狀，只要靠前後支撐即可讓物件在半空移動，且除了搬運待加工的物件，也可移動加
工設備進行加工。單軸機器人則是將線性滑軌與滾珠螺桿模組化，使客戶安裝使用更為便利，單軸
機器人在半導體相關製造非常常見。 
過去的線性傳動元件絕大部分是由日系廠商主導，係因日本製造業對滑軌的需求非常強勁，光是日
本國內就常供不應求。台灣廠商過去受惠國際自動化需求漸增趨勢，日廠無法供應足量產品而漸漸
展露頭角，廠商近年透過在穩定度、精密性、耐久性的提升，不但已可提供與日廠幾乎相同水準的
產品，價格上也更具優勢，為有意導入自動化設備的廠商提供更優質且經濟的選擇。  
近年中國自動化需求逐漸攀升，主因 1)人力成本持續上升；2) 現代人較少加班，平均工作時數較以
往短。中國亦有許多廠商進入線性傳動元件市場，但因中國廠商的技術能力與台、日廠商仍有差距，
且中國廠商多專注於價格優勢而非品質優化，故儘管有中國廠商積極搶市，但受影響僅止於台灣較
不具高階技術能力的供應商，上銀由於技術能力強、整合能力高，對於中國有意引進自動化的廠商
仍是優先選擇。

線性元件亦為寡占市場，上銀市占率排名第二  
目前全球線性元件市場為寡占市場，前三名市占率超過 7 成。上銀 2022 年線性元件市占率成功超
越日商 NSK，目前市佔率為 21%，僅次於日商 THK 的 32%。上銀的後續成長動能將來自於高端線
性元件，主要應用於電動車與半導體產業。上銀相較其他競爭者之優勢主要來自於自製製程壓低成
本帶來的高性價比，上銀產品品質相較低價的中國廠高，而價格較品質相當的日本同業低。

圖 17：線性滑軌示意圖 圖 18：滾珠螺桿示意圖

圖 21：線性滑軌市占率

資料來源：元大投顧

中國進入價格競爭、供應鏈轉移與通縮的下行循環，美、歐、東南亞為發展重點 
自動化產業最近兩次上升循環均與中國有關。第一次為 2016-2018 年，主因中國受惠電子、汽車、
太陽能等需求大幅提升，大量導入自動化設備，整體市場呈現價量齊揚。第二次上升週期為 2020-
2022 年，主因 2020 年 Covid-19 使中國封城、全球供應鏈斷鏈、缺工嚴重下，解封後工業生產加
速帶動自動化需求提升。然在 4Q22 解封後，高通膨、高利率、電動車增速放緩、太陽能供過於求、
房地產泡沫、美中貿易戰等多因素干擾，整體中國經濟成長放緩，連帶影響整體工業設備投入速度
放緩。同時間因中國需求不振導致內部價格競爭加劇，導致行業出現惡性循環。儘管中國政府持續
推出刺激政策，然整體製造工業在 1) 中美貿易戰使供應鏈外移至東南亞趨勢明確；2) 過去 20 年擔
任經濟領頭羊的房地產泡沫化；3) 人口紅利不再，供應鏈外移使消費動能差，景氣通縮。本中心認
為整體自動化市場未來 3-5 年將以中國曝險較低廠商有望於產業中脫穎而出，成長動能將會以美國、
歐洲製造以及東南亞生產為發展重心。

THK Hiwin NSK Others
